in the superscript.
If there is no weight
(\gamma_{2},\partial W)
in the superscript, the
\delta
–terms for the weights
should be understood as
(\delta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\rm o})^{\max\{k+\gamma_{1},0\}}

and
(\delta_{\mathbf{x}}^{\rm o})^{\max\{k+\alpha+\gamma_{1},0\}}

in (
3.1
) and (
3.19
), respectively.
Moreover, when no weight appears in the superscripts of the seminorms
in (
3.1
) and (
3.19
),
it means that neither
\delta^{\rm o}
nor
\delta^{\rm w}
is present.
For a function of one variable defined on
(0,\infty)
,
the weighted norm
\|f\|^{(\gamma_{1};0)}_{k,\alpha;(l);\mathbb{R}^{+}}

is understood in the same as the definition
above with the weight to
\{0\}
and the decay
at infinity.

In the study of Problem
3.2
for a transonic solution

(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
with wedge angle
\theta_{\rm w}
,
the variables in
U

are expected to have different levels of regularity.
Thus, we distinguish these variables by defining

(3.22)

{U_{1}}=(\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0},\rho),\qquad U_{2}=(w,p)\,\,\,\,\mbox{with $w=\frac{\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}^{0}}{\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0}}$},

where
{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}^{0}=(-\sin\theta_{\rm w},\cos\theta_{\rm w})
and

{\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0}=(\cos\theta_{\rm w},\sin\theta_{\rm w})
.
We note that, for the solutions under our consideration,
the denominator in the definition of
w
is strictly positive, since
it is a positive constant for the background solution.

Note that
U_{10}^{+}=(|\mathbf{u}_{0}^{+}|,\rho_{0}^{+})
and
U_{20}^{+}=(0,p_{0}^{+})
are
the corresponding quantities for the background subsonic state.
Moreover,
{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}^{0}
is the interior (for
\Omega_{0}
) unit normal to
{\partial}W_{0}
,
and
{\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0}
is the tangential unit vector to
{\partial}W_{0}
, where
{\partial}W_{0}
and

\Omega_{0}
are defined by (
3.13
) and (
3.17
) for the background solution

(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
,
i.e.
,

\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0}
and
\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}^{0}
are the components
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
of
\mathbf{u}

in the coordinates rotated clockwise
by angle
\theta_{\rm w}
, so that the background downstream flow becomes horizontal.

Theorem 3.1

(Chen-Chen-Feldman
[
25
]
)
.

Let
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
be a constant transonic solution
for the wedge angle
\theta_{\rm w}\in(0,\theta_{\rm w}^{\rm d})
.
There are positive constants
\alpha,\beta,C_{0}
, and
\varepsilon
, depending only on the background
states
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
, such that

(i)

If
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
corresponds to the state on arc
\wideparen{TS}
,
and

(3.23)

\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\Omega^{-}}+\|b^{\prime}-\tan\theta_{\rm w}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{1,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}<\varepsilon,

then
there exist a solution
(U,\sigma)
of
Problem
3.2
(WT)

and a function
U^{\infty}(\mathbf{x})=(u^{\infty}_{1},0,p_{0}^{+},\rho^{\infty})(\mathbf{x})=Z^{\infty}(-x_{1}\sin\theta_{\rm w}+x_{2}\cos\theta_{\rm w})

with
Z^{\infty}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{4}
of form
Z^{\infty}=(z_{1},0,p_{0}^{+},z_{4})

such that
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
defined by (
3.22
) satisfy

\displaystyle\qquad\|U_{1}-U_{1}^{\infty}\|^{(-\alpha;{\partial}W)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta,1);\Omega}+\|U_{2}-U_{20}^{+}\|^{(-\alpha;{O})(-1-\alpha;{\partial}W)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\Omega}+\|\sigma^{\prime}-s_{0}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}+\|Z_{1}^{\infty}-U^{+}_{10}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta);[0,\infty)}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq{}C_{0}\left(\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\Omega^{-}}+\|b^{\prime}-\tan\theta_{\rm w}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{1,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\right),

where we have denoted
U^{\infty}_{1}:=(\mathbf{u}^{\infty}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0},\rho^{\infty})=(u^{\infty}_{1}\cos\theta_{\rm w},\rho^{\infty})

and
Z^{\infty}_{1}:=(z_{1}\cos\theta_{\rm w},z_{4})
;

(ii)

If
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
corresponds to the state on arc
\wideparen{TH}

and

(3.25)

\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(\beta,0);\Omega^{-}}+\|b^{\prime}-\tan\theta_{\rm w}\|^{(-\alpha-1;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}<\varepsilon,

then
there exists a solution
(U,\sigma)
of

Problem
3.2
(ST)
such that
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
defined by (
3.22
) satisfy

(3.26)

\begin{split}&\|U_{1}-U_{10}^{+}\|^{(-1-\alpha;\partial W)}_{2,\alpha;(0,\beta);\Omega}+\|U_{2}-U_{20}^{+}\|^{(-1-\alpha;{O})}_{2,\alpha;(\beta,0);\Omega}+\|\sigma^{\prime}-s_{0}\|^{(-1-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\\[5.69054pt]
&\leq{}C_{0}\left(\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\Omega^{-}}+\|b^{\prime}-\tan\theta_{\rm w}\|^{(-1-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\right).\end{split}

The solution
(U,\sigma)
is unique within the class of solutions
for each of
Problem
3.2
(WT)
and
Problem
3.2
(ST)

when the left-hand sides of
(
3.24
) for
Problem
3.2
(WT)

and (
3.26
)
for
Problem
3.2
(ST)
are less than
C_{0}\varepsilon
correspondingly.

The dependence of constants
\alpha,\beta,C_{0}
, and
\varepsilon
in Theorem
3.1

is as follows:
\alpha
and
\beta
depend on
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})

but are independent of
(C_{0},\varepsilon)
,

C_{0}
depends on
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0},\alpha,\beta)
but is independent of
\varepsilon
,
and
\varepsilon
depends on all
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0},\alpha,\beta,C_{0})
.

The difference in the results of the two problems is that the solution of
Problem
3.2
(WT) has less regularity at corner
{O}

and decays faster with respect to
|\mathbf{x}|
(or the distance from the wedge boundary)
than the solution of Problem
3.2
(ST).

Notice that part (
i
) of Theorem
3.1
gives the asymptotics
of solution
U
as
|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty
within

\Omega
, and
U^{\infty}
is an asymptotic profile.
Moreover, the convergence of
U_{2}

to
U_{2}^{\infty}=U_{20}^{+}
as
|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty

is of polynomial rate
|\mathbf{x}|^{-(\beta+1)}
that
is faster than the convergence rate of
U_{1}
, which is
|\mathbf{x}|^{-\beta}
.
However, as
x_{2}\to\infty
, both
U_{1}
and
U_{2}

decay to
U_{10}^{+}
and
U_{20}^{+}
, respectively,
with the decay rate
x_{2}^{-(\beta+1)}
,
which can be seen by combining the estimates of
the first and last terms on the right-hand side of
(
3.24
) for
U_{1}
.
Part (
ii
) of Theorem
3.1
does not give the asymptotic limit
of
U_{1}
as
|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty
, while
U_{2}
converges
to
U_{20}^{+}
with the decay rate
|\mathbf{x}|^{-\beta}
. Also,
as
x_{2}\to\infty
, both
U_{1}
and
U_{2}

decay to
U_{10}^{+}
and
U_{20}^{+}
, respectively,
with the decay rate
x_{2}^{-\beta}
for part (
ii
).

Furthermore, for both parts (
i
) and (
ii
) of Theorem
3.1
,
the asymptotic profile in the Lagrangian coordinates is given in Theorem
3.3
.

3.2.
Approach I for Problem
3.2
(WT)

We now describe Approach I for solving Problem
3.2
(WT).
We work in the Lagrangian coordinates introduced in (
3.6
).
From the slip condition (
3.15
) on the wedge boundary
{\partial}{W}
, it follows that

{\partial}{W}
is a streamline so that

{\partial}{W}
becomes the half-line

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\{(y_{1},y_{2})\,:\;y_{1}>0,y_{2}=0\}

in the Lagrangian coordinates.
Let
\mathcal{S}=\{y_{2}=\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})\}
be a shock-front. Then,
from equations (
3.7
)–(
3.10
), we can derive the Rankine-Hugoniot
conditions along
\mathcal{S}
:

(3.27)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(y_{1})\big[\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}}\big]=-\big[\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}\big],

(3.28)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(y_{1})\big[u_{1}+\frac{p}{\rho u_{1}}\big]=-\big[\frac{pu_{2}}{u_{1}}\big],

(3.29)

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(y_{1})[\,u_{2}\,]=[\,p\,],

(3.30)

\displaystyle\big[\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{u}|^{2}+\frac{\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)\rho}\big]=0.

The background shock-front in the Lagrangian coordinates is
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\{y_{2}=s_{1}y_{1}\}
with

s_{1}=\rho_{0}^{+}u_{10}^{+}(s_{0}-\tan\theta_{0})>0
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that, in the Lagrangian coordinates, the supersonic solution

U^{-}
exists in domain
\mathbb{D}^{-}
defined by

(3.31)

\mathbb{D}^{-}=\left\{\mathbf{y}\,:\,y_{2}>\frac{s_{1}}{2}y_{1},\,y_{1}>0\right\}.

For a given shock function
\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})
, let

(3.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{-}_{\hat{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{\mathbf{y}:y_{2}>\hat{\sigma}(y_{1}),\,y_{1}>0\big\},

(3.33)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{\mathbf{y}:0<y_{2}<\hat{\sigma}(y_{1}),\,y_{1}>0\big\}.

Then
Approach I
consists of three steps:

1.
Potential function
\phi(\mathbf{y})
.
We first use a potential function to reduce the full Euler equations
(
3.7
)–(
3.10
)
to a scalar second-order nonlinear elliptic PDE in the subsonic region.
This method was first proposed in
[
23
]
in which
the advantage of the conservation properties of the Euler system is taken for the reduction.

More precisely, since
\rho u_{1}\neq 0
in either the supersonic or subsonic region,
it follows from (
3.7
) that
there exists a potential function of the vector field

(\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}},\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}})
such that

(3.34)

D\phi=(\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}},\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}})\qquad\,\,\mbox{with $\phi(\mathbf{0})=0$}.

Equation (
3.10
) implies the Bernoulli law:

(3.35)

\frac{1}{2}q^{2}+\frac{\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)\rho}=B(y_{2}),

where
q=|\mathbf{u}|=\sqrt{u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}}
, and

B=B(y_{2})
is completely determined by the given incoming flow
U^{-}

at the initial position
\mathcal{I}
because of the
Rankine-Hugoniot condition (
3.30
).

From equations (
3.7
)–(
3.10
), we find

(3.36)

\Big(\frac{p}{\rho^{\gamma}}\Big)_{y_{1}}=0,

which implies

(3.37)

p=A(y_{2})\rho^{\gamma}\qquad\,\,\mbox{in the subsonic region $\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}$}.

With equations (
3.34
) and (
3.37
), we can
rewrite the Bernoulli law (
3.35
) as

(3.38)

\frac{\phi_{y_{1}}^{2}+1}{2\phi_{y_{2}}^{2}}+\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}A\rho^{\gamma+1}=B\rho^{2}.

In the subsonic region,
q=|\mathbf{u}|<c=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma p}{\rho}}
. Therefore,
the Bernoulli law (
3.35
) implies

(3.39)

\rho^{\gamma-1}>\frac{2(\gamma-1)B}{\gamma(\gamma+1)A}.

Condition (
3.39
) guarantees that
\rho
can be
solved from (
3.38
) as a smooth function of
(D\phi,A,B)
.

Assume that
A=A(y_{2})
has been determined. Then
(\mathbf{u},p,\rho)
can be
expressed as functions of
D\phi
:

(3.40)

\rho=\rho(D\phi,A,B),\qquad\mathbf{u}=(\frac{1}{\rho\phi_{y_{2}}},\frac{\phi_{y_{1}}}{\rho\phi_{y_{2}}}),\qquad p=A\rho^{\gamma},

since
B=B(y_{2})
is given by the incoming flow.

Similarly, in the supersonic region
\mathbb{D}^{-}
, we employ the corresponding
variables
(\phi^{-},A^{-},B)
to replace
U^{-}
, where
B
is the
same as in the subsonic region because of the Rankine-Hugoniot
condition (
3.30
).

We now choose (
3.9
) to derive a
second-order
nonlinear elliptic equation for
\phi
so that the full Euler
system (
3.7
)–(
3.10
)
is reduced to the following nonlinear PDE in the subsonic region:

(3.41)

\big(N^{1}(D\phi,A,B)\big)_{y_{1}}+\big(N^{2}(D\phi,A,B)\big)_{y_{2}}=0,

where
(N^{1},N^{2})(D\phi,A,B)=(u_{2},p)(D\phi,A,B)

are given by

(3.42)

N^{1}(D\phi,A,B)=\frac{\phi_{y_{1}}}{\phi_{y_{2}}\rho(D\phi,A(y_{2}),B(y_{2}))},\quad N^{2}(D\phi,A,B)=A(y_{2})\big(\rho(D\phi,A(y_{2}),B(y_{2}))\big)^{\gamma}.

Then a careful calculation shows
that

(3.43)

N^{1}_{\phi_{y_{1}}}N^{2}_{\phi_{y_{2}}}-N^{1}_{\phi_{y_{2}}}N^{2}_{\phi_{y_{1}}}=\frac{c^{2}\rho^{2}u_{1}^{2}}{c^{2}-q^{2}}>0

in the subsonic region with
\rho u_{1}\neq 0
.
Therefore, when
\phi
is sufficiently close to
\phi_{0}^{+}
(determined
by the subsonic background state
U_{0}^{+}
) in the
C^{1}
–norm,
equation (
3.41
) is uniformly elliptic, and the Euler system
(
3.7
)–(
3.10
) is reduced to the elliptic
equation (
3.41
) in domain
\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}
,
where
\hat{\sigma}
is the function for the free boundary (transonic shock).

The boundary condition for
\phi
on the wedge boundary
\{y_{2}=0\}
is
derived from the fact that
\phi(y_{1},y_{2})=x_{2}(y_{1},y_{2})
by
(
3.5
)–(
3.6
) and (
3.34
).
Then, recalling that
{\partial}{W}=\{\mathbf{x}\,:\;x_{2}=b(x_{1}),x_{1}>0\}
in the
\mathbf{x}
–coordinates
which is

\{\mathbf{y}\,:\;y_{2}=0,y_{1}>0\}
in the
\mathbf{y}
–coordinates
and using
y_{1}=x_{1}
by (
3.6
), we obtain

(3.44)

\phi(y_{1},0)=b(y_{1}).

The condition on
\mathcal{S}
is derived from the Rankine-Hugoniot
conditions (
3.27
)–(
3.29
). Condition
(
3.27
) is equivalent to the continuity of
\phi
across

\mathcal{S}
:

(3.45)

[\phi]|_{\mathcal{S}}=0,

which, by (
3.34
), gives

(3.46)

\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(y_{1})=-\frac{[\phi_{y_{1}}]}{[\phi_{y_{2}}]}(y_{1},\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})).

Replacing
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(y_{1})
in (
3.28
)–(
3.29
)
by (
3.46
) and using (
3.37
) give rise to the
conditions on
\mathcal{S}
:

(3.47)

\displaystyle G(D\phi,A,U^{-})\equiv[\phi_{y_{1}}]\big[\frac{1}{\rho\phi_{y_{2}}}+A\rho^{\gamma}\phi_{y_{2}}\big]-[\phi_{y_{2}}][A\rho^{\gamma}\phi_{y_{1}}]=0,

(3.48)

\displaystyle H(D\phi,A,U^{-})\equiv[\phi_{y_{1}}][N^{1}]+[\phi_{y_{2}}][N^{2}]=0.

We now combine the above two conditions into the boundary
condition for (
3.41
) by eliminating
A
.
Taking the partial derivative of
G
and
H
with respect
to
A
respectively and making careful calculation, we have

\displaystyle G_{A}

\displaystyle=[\phi_{y_{1}}]\Big(\frac{N^{1}_{A}}{\phi_{y_{1}}}+\phi_{y_{2}}N^{2}_{A}\Big)-[\phi_{y_{2}}]\phi_{y_{1}}N^{2}_{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{2}\rho^{\gamma}(q^{2}+\frac{c^{2}}{\gamma-1})}{u_{1}(c^{2}-q^{2})}\left[\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}}\right]-\frac{\rho^{\gamma-1}}{u_{1}(c^{2}-q^{2})}\left(u_{2}^{2}+\frac{c^{2}-u_{1}^{2}}{\gamma-1}\right)\left[\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}\right]<0,

and

\displaystyle H_{A}=N^{1}_{A}[\phi_{y_{1}}]+N^{2}_{A}[\phi_{y_{2}}]=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\frac{\rho^{\gamma-1}u_{2}}{c^{2}-q^{2}}\left[\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}\right]-\frac{\rho^{\gamma}(q^{2}+\frac{c^{2}}{\gamma-1})}{c^{2}-q^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}}\right]>0,

since
[\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}}]<0
and
u_{2}^{-}
is close to
0
.
Therefore, both equations (
3.47
) and (
3.48
) can be solved
for
A
to obtain

A=g_{1}(D\phi,U^{-})
and
A=g_{2}(D\phi,U^{-})
, respectively.
With these, we obtain our desired condition
on the free boundary (
i.e.
, the shock-front):

(3.49)

\bar{g}(D\phi,U^{-}):=(g_{2}-g_{1})(D\phi,U^{-})=0.

Then the original free boundary problem, Problem
3.2
, is reduced to
the following free boundary problem for the elliptic equation (
3.41
):

Problem 3.3

(Free Boundary Problem)
.

Seek
(\hat{\sigma},\phi,A)
such that
\phi
is a solution of the elliptic
equation (
3.41
) in the region with the fixed boundary condition
(
3.44
) and
conditions (
3.45
) and (
3.47
)–(
3.49
)
on
\mathcal{S}
.

2.
Hodograph transformation and fixed boundary value problem
.
In order to solve the free boundary problem, we employ the
hodograph transformation so that the shock-front becomes a fixed boundary
by using the free boundary conditions (
3.45
) and (
3.49
).
This allows us to find
\phi
for each
A
from an appropriately chosen set.
After that, we only need to perform an iteration for the unknown function
A

to satisfy (
3.47
)–(
3.48
).

Note that the expected solutions in Theorem
3.1
satisfy that

\|U-U^{+}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon
. Then, denoting by

\phi_{0}^{+}
the potential function (
3.34
) for the subsonic background state
U^{+}_{0}
,
we obtain that
\phi
is close to
\phi_{0}^{+}
in
C^{1}
on the closure of the subsonic region.
On the iteration, we consider (and eventually obtain) solutions
U
for which the same property holds.
Thus, we assume that
\phi
is close to
\phi_{0}^{+}
in
C^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}})
below;
see (
3.33
).

We now extend the domain of
\phi^{-}
from
\mathbb{D}^{-}
to the first quadrant
\mathbb{D}^{-}\cup\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}
.
Let

\phi^{-}_{0}=\frac{1}{\rho^{-}_{0}u_{20}^{-}}y_{2},

which is the potential function (
3.34
) for the supersonic background state
U^{-}_{0}
.
Then
\phi^{-}
is close to
\phi^{-}_{0}
in
C^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}^{-}})
since
U^{-}
is close to
U^{-}_{0}
in

L^{\infty}
(and in the stronger norm; see Theorem
3.1
). We can extend
\phi^{-}
into

\mathbb{D}^{-}\cup\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}

so that it remains close to
\phi^{-}_{0}
in
C^{1}
on the closure of
\mathbb{D}^{-}\cup\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}
.
We then use the following partial hodograph transformation:

(3.50)

(y_{1},y_{2})\to(z_{1},z_{2})=(\phi-\phi^{-},y_{2}).

Note that

\partial_{y_{1}}(\phi_{0}^{+}-\phi_{0}^{-})=\frac{u^{+}_{20}}{u^{+}_{10}}>0
by using (
3.34
),
where
(u^{+}_{10},u^{+}_{20})
is the velocity of the background subsonic state
U_{0}^{+}

and the fact that
u^{-}_{20}=0
has been used.
Since
\phi
and
\phi^{-}
are close to
\phi_{0}^{+}
and
\phi_{0}^{-}
in the
C^{1}
–norm respectively,
transformation (
3.50
) is invertible, so that

y_{1}
is a function of
\mathbf{z}:=(z_{1},z_{2})
, denoted as
y_{1}=\varphi(\mathbf{z})
.

Let

\displaystyle M^{1}(D\phi,A,U^{-})=N^{1}(D\phi,A,B)+N^{2}(D\phi,A,B)\frac{[\phi_{y_{2}}]}{[\phi_{y_{1}}]},\quad M^{2}(D\phi,A,U^{-})=\frac{N^{2}(D\phi,A,B)}{[\phi_{y_{1}}]},

and

\displaystyle\overline{M}^{i}(D\varphi,\varphi,A,\mathbf{z})=-M^{i}(\partial_{y_{1}}{\phi^{-}}(\varphi,z_{2})+\frac{1}{\varphi_{z_{1}}},\,\partial_{y_{2}}{\phi^{-}}(\varphi,z_{2})-\frac{\varphi_{z_{2}}}{\varphi_{z_{1}}},A,U^{-}(\varphi,z_{2})),\qquad i=1,2.

Then equation (
3.41
) becomes

(3.51)

\big(\overline{M}^{1}(D\varphi,\varphi,A,\mathbf{z})\big)_{z_{1}}+\big(\overline{M}^{2}(D\varphi,\varphi,A,\mathbf{z})\big)_{z_{2}}=0.

Notice that

\displaystyle\overline{M}^{1}_{\varphi_{z_{1}}}\overline{M}^{2}_{\varphi_{z_{2}}}-\frac{1}{4}\big(\overline{M}^{1}_{\varphi_{z_{2}}}+\overline{M}^{2}_{\varphi_{z_{1}}}\big)^{2}=[\phi_{y_{1}}]^{2}\big(N^{1}_{\phi_{y_{1}}}N^{2}_{\phi_{y_{2}}}-(N^{1}_{\phi_{y_{2}}})^{2}\big)>0,

which implies that equation (
3.51
) is uniformly elliptic, for any solution

\varphi
that is close to
\varphi_{0}^{+}
(determined by (
3.50
) with
\phi=\phi_{0}^{+}
)
in the
C^{1}
–norm.

Under transform (
3.50
), the unknown shock-front
\mathcal{S}
becomes a fixed boundary, which
is the
z_{2}
-axis,
where we have used that
\phi
is close in
C^{1}
to
\phi_{0}^{+}

in
\overline{\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}

and to
\phi_{0}^{-}
in
\overline{\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-}}
in order
to conclude that

\phi
is Lipschitz across
\mathcal{S}
from (
3.34
) and then that
\phi=\phi^{-}

on
\mathcal{S}
but
\phi\neq\phi^{-}
in
\overline{\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}\setminus\mathcal{S}
.
Along the
z_{2}
-axis, condition
(
3.49
) is now

\displaystyle\tilde{g}(D\varphi,\varphi,\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle:=\bar{g}(\partial_{y_{1}}{\phi^{-}}(\varphi,z_{2})+\frac{1}{\varphi_{z_{1}}},\partial_{y_{2}}{\phi^{-}}(\varphi,z_{2})-\frac{\varphi_{z_{2}}}{\varphi_{z_{1}}},U^{-}(\varphi,z_{2}))

(3.52)

\displaystyle=0\qquad\quad\mbox{on }\{z_{1}=0,\,z_{2}>0\}.

We also convert condition (
3.48
) into the
\mathbf{z}
–coordinates:
Along the
z_{2}
-axis,

(3.53)

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}(D\varphi,\varphi,A,\mathbf{z}):={H}(\partial_{y_{1}}{\phi^{-}}(\varphi,z_{2})+\frac{1}{\varphi_{z_{1}}},\,\partial_{y_{2}}{\phi^{-}}(\varphi,z_{2})-\frac{\varphi_{z_{2}}}{\varphi_{z_{1}}},A,U^{-}(\varphi,z_{2}))=0.

The condition on the
z_{1}
-axis can be derived from
(
3.44
) as follows: Restricted on
z_{2}=0
, the
coordinate transformation (
3.50
) becomes

z_{1}=b(y_{1})-\phi_{-}(y_{1},0).

Then
y_{1}
can be expressed in terms of
z_{1}
as
y_{1}=\widetilde{b}(z_{1})
so that

\varphi(z_{1},0)=y_{1}
becomes

(3.54)

\varphi(z_{1},0)=\widetilde{b}(z_{1})\qquad\mbox{ on $\mathcal{L}_{1}:=\{z_{2}=0,\,z_{1}>0\}$}.

Therefore, the original wedge problem has now been reduced to
the following problem on the first quadrant
\mathbb{Q}
.

Problem 3.4

(Fixed Boundary Value Problem)
.

Seek
(\varphi,A)
such that
\varphi
is a solution of the second-order nonlinear elliptic equation (
3.51
)
in the unbounded domain
\mathbb{Q}
with the boundary conditions (
3.2
)
and (
3.54
), and such that (
3.53
) holds.

3.
Solution to the fixed boundary value problem – Problem
3.4
.
Through the shock polar,
we can determine the values of
U
at the origin so that
A(0)
is fixed, depending
on the values of
U^{-}(\mathbf{0})
and
b^{\prime}(0)
.
Then we solve
(
3.53
) to obtain a unique solution

\tilde{A}=h(\mathbf{z},\phi,D\phi)
that defines the iteration map.

This is achieved by the following fixed point argument.
Consider a Banach space:

X=\{\mathcal{A}\,:\,\mathcal{A}(0)=0,\,\|\mathcal{A}\|_{1,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}^{(-\alpha);\{0\}}<\infty\}.

Then we define our iteration map
\mathcal{J}:X\longrightarrow X

through the following:

First, we define a smooth cutoff
function
\chi
on
[0,\infty)
such that

\chi(s)=\begin{cases}1&\,\,\mbox{for $0\leq s<1$},\\[1.42262pt]
0&\,\,\mbox{for $s>2$}.\\
\end{cases}

Set

(3.55)

A(0):=t(\omega(0),b^{\prime}(0))\qquad\mbox{for $\omega=U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}$},

where
t
is a function
determined by the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions
(
3.47
)–(
3.48
).
Then we define
w_{t}(z_{2})
as

(3.56)

w_{t}(z_{2}):=A^{+}_{0}+\big(t(\omega(0),b^{\prime}(0))-A^{+}_{0}\big)\chi(z_{2}),

where
A^{+}_{0}=\frac{p_{0}^{+}}{(\rho_{0}^{+})^{\gamma}}
.

Consider any
A=A(z_{2})
so that
A-w_{t}\in X
satisfying

(3.57)

\|A-A_{0}^{+}\|_{1,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}^{(-\alpha);\{0\}}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon

for some fixed constant
C_{0}>0
.
With this
A
, we solve equation (
3.51
) for
\varphi=\varphi_{A}
in
the unbounded domain
\mathbb{Q}
with the boundary conditions (
3.2
)
and (
3.54
), and with the asymptotic condition
\varphi\to\varphi^{\infty}
as
\mathbf{x}\to\infty
,
where the limit is understood in the appropriate sense,
\varphi^{\infty}
is the solution of

(3.58)

z_{1}=(\phi^{\infty}-\phi^{-})(\varphi^{\infty},z_{2}),

with
\phi^{\infty}=\frac{u_{20}^{+}}{u_{10}^{+}}y_{1}+l(y_{2})
,
and

l(y_{2})
is determined by the Bernoulli law (
3.38
),
via replacing

\phi
and
\rho
by their asymptotic values

\phi^{\infty}
and
\rho^{\infty}(y_{2})=\big(\frac{p_{0}^{+}}{A(y_{2})}\big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

and noting that
B=B(y_{2})
is determined by the upstream state
U^{-}
.
More specifically, we show the existence of a solution
\varphi

of (
3.51
)–(
3.2
) and (
3.54
)
in the set:

\Sigma_{\delta}=\big\{\varphi:\,\|\varphi-\varphi^{\infty}\|_{2,\alpha;(\beta,0);\mathbb{Q}}^{(-1-\alpha);\mathcal{L}_{1}}\leq\delta\big\}\qquad\mbox{for sufficiently small $\delta>0$},

which is a compact and convex subset of the Banach space:

\mathcal{B}=\big\{\varphi\,:\,\|\varphi-\varphi^{\infty}\|_{2,\alpha^{\prime};(\beta^{\prime},0);\mathbb{Q}}^{(-1-\alpha^{\prime});\mathcal{L}_{1}}<\infty\big\}\qquad\,\,\mbox{with $0<\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha$ and $0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta$}.

For
\varphi\in\Sigma_{\delta}
, equation (
3.51
)
is uniformly elliptic if
\delta>0
is small.
This allows us to solve the problem for
\varphi=\varphi_{A}\in\Sigma_{\delta}

by the Schauder fixed point theorem
if the perturbation is small,
i.e.
,
if
\varepsilon
is small in (
3.57
) and the conditions of Theorem
3.1
.
Then, with this
\varphi=\varphi_{A}
, we solve (
3.53
) to obtain
a unique
\tilde{A}
that defines the iteration map
J
by
\mathcal{J}(A-w_{t}):=\tilde{A}-w_{t}
.

Finally, by the implicit function theorem,
we prove that
\mathcal{J}
has a fixed point
A-w_{t}
, for which
A
satisfies
(
3.57
).

For more details for this approach, see Chen-Chen-Feldman
[
23
,
24
]
.
This approach can also be applied to Problem
3.2
(ST);
see
[
112
]
for the case when the wedge angle is sufficiently small.

3.3.
Approach II for Problem
3.2
(ST) and Problem
3.2
(WT)

We now describe the second approach, Approach II.
It allows us to handle both cases in
Theorem
3.1
: Problem
3.2
(WT) and Problem
3.2
(ST).
In particular, for Problem
3.2
(WT),
this approach yields a better asymptotic decay rate, as stated in (
3.24
).

It is convenient to rotate the
\mathbf{x}
–coordinates clockwise
by the wedge angle
\theta_{\rm w}
, so that the background downstream flow becomes horizontal, as
discussed in the paragraph before Theorem
3.1
.
We still use the same notations in the rotated coordinates when no confusion arises;
in particular, we write
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
and
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},u_{2})
in the rotated basis.
Then, in the new coordinates,

(3.59)

\frac{u_{20}^{-}}{u_{10}^{-}}=-\tan\theta_{\rm w},\quad U_{0}^{-}=(u_{10}^{-},\,-u_{10}^{-}\tan\theta_{\rm w},\,p_{0}^{-},\,\rho_{0}^{-}),\quad U_{0}^{+}=(u_{10}^{+},\,0,\,p_{0}^{+},\,\rho_{0}^{+}).

Since the velocity components
(u_{1},u_{2})
are now in the basis

({\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0},{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}^{0})
,

i.e.
,
u_{1}=\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\tau}}_{\rm w}^{0}
and
u_{2}=\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}^{0}
,
we see that, by (
3.22
),

(3.60)

{U_{1}}=(u_{1},\rho),\qquad U_{2}=(w,p)\,\,\,\mbox{with $w=\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}$}

in the new coordinates.
Furthermore, we obtain from (
3.13
)
and (
3.23
) or (
3.25
) with small
\varepsilon

that, in the rotated coordinates,

(3.61)

\partial{W}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,x_{2}=b_{\rm rot}(x_{1}),\ b_{\rm rot}(0)=0\},

and function
b_{\rm rot}(x_{1})
satisfies the estimates in (
3.63
)
or (
3.65
) below, respectively, with
C\varepsilon

instead of
\varepsilon

when
\varepsilon
is small, where
C
depends only on
b(\cdot)
.
For the background solution,

b_{{\rm rot},0}=0
,
i.e.
,
\partial{W}_{0}
is the positive
x_{1}
-axis.

We now construct a solution with a shock-front
\mathcal{S}
expressed as
(
3.16
) in the rotated coordinates with a
function

\tilde{\sigma}(x_{1})
. The background shock
is now expressed as
\mathcal{S}_{0}:=\{x_{2}=\tilde{\sigma}_{0}(x_{1})\,:\,x_{1}>0\}
for

\tilde{\sigma}_{0}(x_{1}):=\tilde{s}_{0}x_{1}
, where
\tilde{s}_{0}=\tan(\arctan s_{0}-\theta_{\rm w})
.
Then the subsonic region of the solution has the form:

(3.62)

\Omega=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,:\,b_{\rm rot}(x_{1})<x_{2}<\tilde{\sigma}(x_{1}),x_{1}>0\}.

We can assume that the upstream steady supersonic smooth solution
U^{-}(\mathbf{x})
exists
in region
\Omega^{-}=\{\mathbf{x}\,:\,\frac{\tilde{s}_{0}}{2}x_{1}<x_{2}<2\tilde{s}_{0}x_{1},x_{1}\geq 0\}
,
beyond the background shock, but is still close to
U_{0}^{-}
.
Moreover, in part (
i
) of Theorem
3.1
,

U^{\infty}
is independent of
x_{1}
and
U^{\infty}=Z^{\infty}
in the rotated
coordinates.

More specifically, we establish the following theorem in the rotated coordinates:

Theorem 3.2

(Chen-Chen-Feldman
[
25
]
)
.

Let
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
, given by (
3.59
),
be a constant transonic solution
for the wedge angle
\theta_{\rm w}\in(0,\theta_{\rm w}^{\rm d})
.
There are positive constants
\alpha,\beta,C_{0}
, and
\varepsilon
depending only on the background
states
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
such that

(i)

If
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
corresponds to the state on arc
\wideparen{TS}

and

(3.63)

\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\Omega^{-}}+\|b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{1,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}<\varepsilon,

then there exist a solution
(U,\tilde{\sigma})
of
Problem
3.2
(WT)

and a function

U^{\infty}(x_{2})=(u^{\infty}_{1}(x_{2}),0,p_{0}^{+},\rho^{\infty}(x_{2}))

so that
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
defined by (
3.22
) satisfy

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\,\,\|U_{1}-U_{1}^{\infty}\|^{(-\alpha;{\partial}W)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta,1);\Omega}+\|U_{2}-U_{20}^{+}\|^{(-\alpha;{O})(-1-\alpha;{\partial}W)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\Omega}+\|\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}-\tilde{s}_{0}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}+\|U_{1}^{\infty}-U^{+}_{10}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta);[0,\infty)}

(3.64)

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\,\,\leq{}C_{0}\left(\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\Omega^{-}}+\|b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{1,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\right),

where
U^{\infty}_{1}=(u^{\infty}_{1},\rho^{\infty})
.

(ii)

If
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
corresponds to the state on arc
\wideparen{TH}
,
and

(3.65)

\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(\beta,0);\Omega^{-}}+\|b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}\|^{(-\alpha-1;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}<\varepsilon,

then
there exists a solution
(U,\tilde{\sigma})
of

Problem
3.2
(ST)
so that
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
defined by (
3.22
) satisfy

(3.66)

\begin{split}&\|U_{1}-U_{10}^{+}\|^{(-1-\alpha;\partial W)}_{2,\alpha;(0,\beta);\Omega}+\|U_{2}-U_{20}^{+}\|^{(-1-\alpha;{O})}_{2,\alpha;(\beta,0);\Omega}+\|\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}-\tilde{s}_{0}\|^{(-1-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\\
&\leq{}C_{0}\left(\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\Omega^{-}}+\|b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}\|^{(-1-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\right).\end{split}

The solution
(U,\tilde{\sigma})
is unique within the class of solutions
for each of
Problem
3.2
(WT)
and
Problem
3.2
(ST)

when the left-hand sides of
(
3.24
) for
Problem
3.2
(WT)
and (
3.26
)
for
Problem
3.2
(ST)
are less than
C_{0}\varepsilon
correspondingly.

Clearly, Theorem
3.1
follows from Theorem
3.2
if
\varepsilon
is small
so that, from the estimates of
\tilde{\sigma}
in (
3.64
) or
(
3.66
), the shock remains a graph of
C^{1}
function:
x_{2}=\sigma(x_{1})

after rotating the coordinates back.

To prove Theorem
3.2
,
we work in the Lagrangian coordinates (
3.6
) defined for the rotated coordinates
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
.
Then, as in the previous case, using the fact that the wedge boundary

{\partial}{W}
is a streamline due to the slip condition (
3.15
) on
{\partial}{W}
,
we obtain that, in the present Lagrangian coordinates,

{\partial}{W}
becomes the half-line:

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\{(y_{1},y_{2})\,:\;y_{1}>0,y_{2}=0\}.

The background shock-front
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is now given by

\mathcal{S}_{0}=\{y_{2}=s_{1}y_{1},y_{1}>0\}
with
s_{1}=\rho_{0}^{+}u_{10}^{+}\tilde{s}_{0}
.
We can assume that, in the Lagrangian coordinates, the supersonic solution

U^{-}
exists in domain
\mathbb{D}^{-}
defined by (
3.31
).
Shock
\mathcal{S}
is given by

y_{2}=\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})
for
y_{1}>0
, where function
\hat{\sigma}
differs from the one in Approach I
because the Lagrangian coordinates are now defined differently.
The supersonic region
\mathbb{D}^{-}_{\hat{\sigma}}
and the subsonic region
\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}

of the solution are given by (
3.32
) and (
3.33
) respectively,
with the present function
\hat{\sigma}
.

We first present the existence and estimates of the solution in the Lagrangian coordinates:

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
be a constant transonic solution
for the wedge angle
\theta_{\rm w}\in(0,\theta_{\rm w}^{\rm d})
.
There are positive constants
\alpha,\beta,C_{0}
, and
\varepsilon
depending only on the background
states
(U^{-}_{0},U^{+}_{0})
such that, if
\partial W
in (
3.61
) and
U^{-}
satisfy

(i)

(
3.63
)
for
Problem
3.2
(WT)
,

(ii)

(
3.65
)
for
Problem
3.2
(ST)
,

then there exist a transonic shock
\mathcal{S}_{L}=\{y_{2}=\hat{\sigma}(y_{1}),\,y_{1}>0\}

and a subsonic solution
U=U(\mathbf{y})
of (
3.7
)–(
3.10
) in

\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}
,
satisfying the Rankine-Hugoniot
conditions (
3.27
)–(
3.30
) along
\mathcal{S}_{L}
with
U^{-}
expressed in the Lagrangian coordinates
in
\mathbb{D}^{-}_{\hat{\sigma}}
and the slip condition
w_{|\mathcal{L}_{1}}=b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}
,
as well as there exists a
function
\mathcal{U}^{\infty}(y_{2})=(u_{1}^{\infty}(y_{2}),0,p_{0}^{+},\rho^{\infty}(y_{2}))
,
such that
U(\mathbf{y})
satisfies the following estimates:

(i)

For
Problem
3.2
(WT)
,

\displaystyle\qquad\|U_{1}-\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\infty}\|^{(-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}+\|U_{2}-U_{20}^{+}\|^{(-\alpha;{O})(-1-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}+\|\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}-s_{1}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}+\|\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\infty}-U^{+}_{10}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}

(3.67)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq{}C_{0}\left(\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-}}+\|b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{1,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\right);

(ii)

For

Problem
3.2
(ST)
,

\displaystyle\qquad\|U_{1}-\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\infty}\|^{(-1-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(\beta,0);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}+\|U_{2}-U_{20}^{+}\|^{(-1-\alpha;{O})}_{2,\alpha;(\beta,0);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}+\|\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}-s_{1}\|^{(-1-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}+\|\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\infty}-U^{+}_{10}\|^{(-1-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}

(3.68)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq{}C_{0}\left(\|U^{-}-U^{-}_{0}\|_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-}}+\|b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}\|^{(-1-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\right),

where
\mathcal{U}^{\infty}_{1}(y_{2}):=(u_{1}^{\infty}(y_{2}),\rho^{\infty}(y_{2}))
.

The solution
U
is unique within the class of solutions
for each of
Problem
3.2
(WT)

and
Problem
3.2
(ST)
when the left-hand sides of
(
3.67
) for
Problem
3.2
(WT)

and (
3.68
) for
Problem
3.2
(ST)

are less than
C_{0}\varepsilon
correspondingly.

We remark that function
\mathcal{U}^{\infty}(y_{2})
can be understood as the asymptotic limit of
U(\mathbf{y})
as
y_{1}\to\infty
.

Now we describe the proof of Theorem
3.3
, which is the main part of
Approach II
.
Rewrite system (
3.7
)–(
3.10
) into the
following nondivergence form for
U=(\mathbf{u},p,\rho)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
:

(3.69)

A(U)U_{y_{1}}^{\top}+B(U)U_{y_{2}}^{\top}=0,

where

\displaystyle A(U)=\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}^{2}}&0&0&-\frac{1}{\rho^{2}u_{1}}\\[4.2679pt]
1-\frac{p}{\rho u_{1}^{2}}&0&\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}}&-\frac{p}{\rho^{2}u_{1}}\\[4.2679pt]
0&1&0&0\\[2.84526pt]
u_{1}&u_{2}&\frac{\gamma}{(\gamma-1)\rho}&-\frac{\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)\rho^{2}}\end{bmatrix},\quad B(U)=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}^{2}}&-\frac{1}{u_{1}}&0&0\\[4.2679pt]
\frac{pu_{2}}{u_{1}^{2}}&-\frac{p}{u_{1}}&-\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}&0\\[4.2679pt]
0&0&1&0\\[2.84526pt]
0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}.

Solving
\det(\lambda A-B)=0
for
\lambda
, we obtain four eigenvalues:

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0\,\mbox{(real)},\qquad\lambda_{j}=-\frac{c\rho}{c^{2}-u_{1}^{2}}\big(cu_{2}+(-1)^{j}u_{1}\sqrt{c^{2}-q^{2}}i\big)\,\,\mbox{for $j=3,4$ \, (complex)},

where
q=\sqrt{u_{1}^{2}+u_{2}^{2}}<c
in the subsonic region and
i=\sqrt{-1}
.
The corresponding left eigenvectors are

\displaystyle{\bf l}_{1}=(0,0,0,1),\qquad{\bf l}_{2}=(-pu_{1},u_{1},u_{2},-1),

\displaystyle{\bf l}_{3,4}=(\frac{p(\gamma p-\rho u_{1}^{2})}{(\gamma-1)\rho u_{1}}\lambda_{3,4}+\frac{\gamma p^{2}u_{2}}{(\gamma-1)u_{1}},\,-(u_{1}+\frac{\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)\rho u_{1}})\lambda_{3,4}-\frac{\gamma pu_{2}}{(\gamma-1)u_{1}},\,\frac{\gamma p}{\gamma-1}-u_{2}\lambda_{3,4},\,\lambda_{3,4}).

Then

(i)

Multiplying equations (
3.69
) from the left by
{\bf l}_{1}
leads to
the same equation (
3.10
).
This, together with the Rankine-Hugoniot condition (
3.30
), implies the Bernoulli law (
3.35
)
to be held in both supersonic and subsonic domains, as well as across the shock-front.
Therefore,
B(y_{2})
can be computed from the upstream flow

U^{-}
. If
u_{1}
is a small perturbation of
u_{10}^{+}
, then
u_{1}>0
. Therefore, we can solve (
3.35
) for
u_{1}
:

(3.70)

u_{1}=\frac{\sqrt{2\big(B-\frac{2\gamma p}{(\gamma-1)\rho}\big)}}{\sqrt{1+w^{2}}}\qquad\mbox{with $w=\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}$}.

(ii)

Multiplying system (
3.69
) from the left by
{\bf l}_{2}
also gives (
3.36
).

(iii)

Multiplying equations (
3.69
) from the left by
{\bf l}_{3}

and separating the real and imaginary parts of the equation lead to the elliptic system:

(3.71)

\begin{split}&D_{R}w+hD_{I}p=0,\\
&D_{I}w-hD_{R}p=0,\end{split}

where
D_{R}={\partial}_{y_{1}}+\lambda_{R}{\partial}_{y_{2}},\,D_{I}=\lambda_{I}{\partial}_{y_{2}},\,\lambda_{R}=-\frac{c^{2}\rho u_{2}}{c^{2}-u_{1}^{2}},\,\lambda_{I}=\frac{c\rho u_{1}\sqrt{c^{2}-q^{2}}}{c^{2}-u_{1}^{2}}
,
and
h=\frac{\sqrt{c^{2}-q^{2}}}{c\rho u_{1}^{2}}
.

Therefore, system (
3.7
)–(
3.10
) is
decomposed into (
3.70
)–(
3.71
).

We solve this problem by iteration.
Given
U^{-}
that is close to
U^{-}_{0}
as defined in Theorem
3.3
,
we solve the problem for
U
in the Lagrangian coordinates.
However, since
\mathcal{U}^{\infty}
is not known, we cannot directly
solve Problem
3.2
(WT) for
U
satisfying (
3.67
),
or Problem
3.2
(ST) for
U
satisfying
(
3.68
).
Instead, we solve Problem
3.2
(ST) for
U
that is close to
U_{0}^{+}
as in
(
3.26
), and Problem
3.2
(WT) for
U
in
similar norms with appropriate growths, but using these norms in the Lagrangian coordinates
(more precisely, the
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},z_{2})
–coordinates defined by (
3.75
) below).
Note that these norms are weaker than the ones in (
3.67
) or
(
3.68
) respectively; in particular, they do not determine
any limit for
U_{1}=(u_{1},\rho)
as
|\mathbf{y}|\to\infty
within the subsonic region.
On the other hand, these norms determine that

(w,p)
have the limit:
(0,p_{0}^{+})
at infinity within the subsonic region;
this asymptotic condition is sufficient to make the iteration problem well-defined
(in fact, we use only the asymptotic decay of
w
)
and to obtain the existence and uniqueness for the iteration problem.
After the unique solution
U
of the problem stated in Theorem
3.3

is obtained by iteration,
we identify
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\infty}=(\rho^{\infty},u_{1}^{\infty})

and show the faster convergence of
(\rho,u_{1})
to
(\rho^{\infty},u_{1}^{\infty})
,
which lead to (
3.67
) and
(
3.68
), respectively.
Note that, in the estimates discussed above,

U-U_{0}^{+}
(rather than
U
itself) lies in the weighted spaces (
3.21
). For this
reason, it is convenient to perform the iteration in terms of

(3.72)

\delta U_{1}=U_{1}-U^{+}_{10},\qquad\delta U_{2}=U_{2}-U^{+}_{20},\qquad\delta\hat{\sigma}=\hat{\sigma}-\hat{\sigma}_{0}=\hat{\sigma}-s_{1}y_{1},

where
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are defined by (
3.60
).
Then we follow the steps below to solve this problem:

1.
Introduce a linear boundary value problem for the iteration.

For a given shock-front
\hat{\sigma}
, the subsonic domain
\mathbb{D}^{\hat{\sigma}}
is fixed, depending on
\hat{\sigma}
.
We make the coordinate transformation
to transform the domain from
\mathbb{D}^{\hat{\sigma}}
to
\mathbb{D}
,
where
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}^{\hat{\sigma}_{0}}
with
\hat{\sigma}_{0}(y_{2})=s_{1}y_{1}
is the domain corresponding to the background solution:

(3.73)

\mathbb{D}=\big\{\mathbf{y}:0<y_{2}<s_{1}y_{1}\big\}

with
\partial\mathbb{D}=\overline{\mathcal{L}_{1}}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}
, where

(3.74)

\mathcal{L}_{1}=\{(y_{1},y_{2})\,:\;y_{1}>0,y_{2}=0\},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{2}=\{(y_{1},y_{2})\,:\;y_{1}>0,y_{2}=s_{1}y_{1}\}.

This transformation is:

(3.75)

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2})\,\to\,\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},z_{2}):=(y_{1},y_{2}-\delta\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})),

where
\delta\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})=\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})-\hat{\sigma}_{0}(y_{1})
.
In the
\mathbf{z}
–coordinates,
\mathcal{L}_{1}
corresponds to
\partial W
, and

\mathcal{L}_{2}
corresponds to
\partial\mathcal{S}
. Also,

U(\mathbf{y})
becomes
U_{\hat{\sigma}}(\mathbf{z})
depending on
\hat{\sigma}
.
Then the upstream flow
U^{-}
involves an unknown variable explicitly depending on
\hat{\sigma}
:

(3.76)

U^{-}_{\hat{\sigma}}(\mathbf{z})=U^{-}(z_{1},z_{2}+\delta\hat{\sigma}(z_{1})),

where
U^{-}
is the given upstream flow in the
\mathbf{y}
–coordinates.
Equations (
3.71
) in
\mathbb{D}
in the
\mathbf{z}
-coordinates are:

(3.77)

\begin{cases}\widetilde{D}_{R}w+h\widetilde{D}_{I}p=0,\\
\widetilde{D}_{I}w-h\widetilde{D}_{R}p=0,\end{cases}

where
\widetilde{D}_{R}=\partial_{z_{1}}+(\lambda_{R}-\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\partial_{z_{2}}

and
\widetilde{D}_{I}=\lambda_{I}\partial_{z_{2}}
.
Since
U_{0}^{+}
is a constant vector and
w_{0}^{+}=0
,
the same system holds for
(\delta p,\delta w)
,
where we have used notation (
3.72
).
Moreover, since the iteration:

(\delta U,\delta w)\to(\delta\tilde{U},\delta\tilde{w})

is considered, we use
U:=U_{0}^{+}+\delta U

to determine the coefficients in (
3.77
) and

(\delta\tilde{p},\delta\tilde{w})
for the unknown functions.
Thus, we have

(3.78)

\begin{cases}\widetilde{D}_{R}\delta\tilde{w}+h\widetilde{D}_{I}\delta\tilde{p}=0,\\
\widetilde{D}_{I}\delta\tilde{w}-h\widetilde{D}_{R}\delta\tilde{p}=0.\end{cases}

We use system (
3.78
) in
\mathbb{D}
as a linear system for the iteration.

In the
\mathbf{z}
–coordinates, the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions (
3.27
)–(
3.30
) keep the same form,
except that
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(y_{1})
is replaced by
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(z_{1})
and
U^{-}
is replaced by
U^{-}_{\hat{\sigma}}
along line
z_{2}=s_{1}z_{1}
.
Among the four Rankine-Hugoniot conditions, (
3.30
) is used in the Bernoulli law.
From condition (
3.29
), we have

(3.79)

\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}(z_{1})=\frac{[p]}{[u_{1}w]}(z_{1},s_{1}z_{1}),

which is used to update the shock-front later.
Now, because of (
3.70
), we can use
\bar{U}=(w,p,\rho)
as the unknown variables along
z_{2}=s_{1}z_{1}
.
Using (
3.79
) to eliminate
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
in conditions (
3.27
)–(
3.28
) gives

(3.80)

\displaystyle G_{1}(U_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-},\bar{U}):=[p]\Big[\frac{1}{\rho u_{1}}\Big]+[w][u_{1}w]=0,

(3.81)

\displaystyle G_{2}(U_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-},\bar{U}):=[p]\Big[u_{1}+\frac{p}{\rho u_{1}}\Big]+[pw][u_{1}w]=0,

on
\mathcal{L}_{2}
. We use conditions (
3.80
)–(
3.81
)
to define the linear conditions for the iteration:

\bar{U}\to\widetilde{\bar{U}}
such that, at a fixed point
\bar{U}=\widetilde{\bar{U}}
,
these iteration conditions imply that the original conditions
(
3.80
)–(
3.81
) hold. Specifically, we define the conditions:

(3.82)

\nabla_{\bar{U}}G_{i}(U_{0}^{-},\bar{U}_{0}^{+})\cdot\delta\widetilde{\bar{U}}=\nabla_{\bar{U}}G_{i}(U_{0}^{-},\bar{U}_{0}^{+})\cdot\delta\bar{U}-G_{i}(U_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-},\bar{U})\qquad\mbox{on $\mathcal{L}_{2}$}

for
i=1,2
, which can be written as

(3.83)

b_{i1}\delta\tilde{w}+b_{i2}\delta\tilde{p}+b_{i3}\delta\tilde{\rho}=g_{i}(U_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-},\bar{U})\qquad\,\,\mbox{on $\mathcal{L}_{2}$},

where

(b_{i1},b_{i2},b_{i3}):=\nabla_{\bar{U}}G_{i}(U_{0}^{-},\bar{U}_{0}^{+})
and

g_{i}(U_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-},\bar{U}):=\nabla_{\bar{U}}G_{i}(U_{0}^{-},\bar{U}_{0}^{+})\cdot\delta\bar{U}-G_{i}(U_{\hat{\sigma}}^{-},\bar{U})\qquad\mbox{for $i=1,2$}.

Since there are two conditions in (
3.83
),
i=1,2
, we can eliminate
\delta\tilde{\rho}
to obtain

(3.84)

\delta\tilde{w}+b_{1}\delta\tilde{p}=g_{3}\qquad\,\,\mbox{on $\mathcal{L}_{2}$},

where

(3.85)

b_{1}=\frac{b_{12}b_{23}-b_{22}b_{13}}{b_{11}b_{23}-b_{21}b_{13}},\qquad g_{3}=\frac{b_{23}g_{1}-b_{13}g_{2}}{b_{11}b_{23}-b_{21}b_{13}}

with

\displaystyle b_{11}b_{23}-b_{21}b_{13}=(-u_{20}^{-})[p_{0}]\left(\frac{\gamma p_{0}^{+}}{(\gamma-1)(\rho_{0}^{+})^{2}u_{10}^{+}}+\frac{p_{0}^{-}}{u_{10}^{-}}\big(\frac{1}{(\rho_{0}^{+})^{2}}+\frac{\gamma p_{0}^{+}}{(\gamma-1)(\rho_{0}^{+})^{3}(u_{10}^{+})^{2}}\big)\right)>0.

Figure 3.3.
The shock polar in the
(w,p)
–variables (
cf
.
[
25
]
)

Notice that the shock polar is a one-parameter curve determined by the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions.
If
p
is used as the parameter, by equation (
3.84
),
we obtain that
\delta w=-b_{1}\delta p+g_{3}(\delta p)
, which shows that
-b_{1}\delta p
is the linear term and
g_{3}(\delta p)
is the higher order term.
We know from Fig.
3.3
that
w(p)
is decreasing in
p
on arc
\wideparen{TH}

and increasing on
\wideparen{TS}
.
Therefore, it is easy to see that

(3.86)

b_{1}>0
corresponds to the state on arc
\wideparen{TH}
,

b_{1}<0
to
\wideparen{TS}
, and
b_{1}=0
at the tangent point
T
.

This difference in the sign of
b_{1}
is the reason for the different rates of decay at infinity and near the origin
in the two different cases (i) and (ii) of Theorems
3.1
and
3.3
.

It can be checked that

b_{13}=-[p_{0}]\left(\frac{p_{0}^{+}}{(\rho_{0}^{+})^{2}u_{10}^{+}}+\frac{\gamma p_{0}^{+}}{(\gamma-1)(\rho_{0}^{+})^{3}(u_{10}^{+})^{3}}\right)<0.

Thus, condition (
3.83
) for
i=1
can be rewritten as

(3.87)

\delta\tilde{\rho}=g_{4}-b_{2}\delta\tilde{w}-b_{3}\delta\tilde{p}\qquad\mbox{on }\mathcal{L}_{2},

where
g_{4}=\frac{g_{1}}{b_{13}},b_{2}=\frac{b_{11}}{b_{13}}
, and
b_{3}=\frac{b_{12}}{b_{13}}
.

We notice that conditions (
3.84
)–(
3.87
) are equivalent to conditions (
3.83
) for
i=1,2
.
The boundary condition on
\mathcal{L}_{1}
comes from the slip condition (
3.15
) on

\partial W
.
Specifically, using (
3.15
) and (
3.61
),
we obtain that
w=b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}
on
{\partial}{W}
. Then, in
the
\mathbf{z}
–coordinates, this must hold on
\mathcal{L}_{1}
.
Also, for the background solution,
b_{\rm rot}=0

by (
3.61
). Then we prescribe

(3.88)

\delta\tilde{w}=b_{\rm rot}^{\prime}\qquad\mbox{on }\mathcal{L}_{1}.

2.
Design the iteration map
\mathcal{Q}
and prove the existence of a fixed point for
\mathcal{Q}
.
We perform the iteration in terms of
\delta U_{k}
,
k=1,2
, and
\delta\hat{\sigma}
as
defined by (
3.72
), in the
\mathbf{z}
-coordinates defined in
(
3.75
). In fact, for
\hat{\sigma}
, we only need
\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
since
\hat{\sigma}(0)=0
,

i.e.
, the shock is attached
to the wedge vertex. Note also that

\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}=\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}-s_{1}
.
We thus denote
V=(U_{1},U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})
and
perform the following iteration:
\delta V\to\delta\tilde{V}
.
For a given
\delta V
, we determine
V:=\delta V+V_{0}^{+}
.
Then we find
\tilde{V}
by solving the linear system (
3.78
)
in
\mathbb{D}
with the boundary conditions (
3.84
) and (
3.88
),
to determine
(\tilde{w},\tilde{p})

and then determine

u_{1}
from
(
3.70
) and
\rho
from (
3.36
)
(which holds in the
\mathbf{z}
–coordinates without change),
and the boundary condition
(
3.87
).
The final step is to use solution
(\delta u_{1},\delta\rho,\delta w,\delta p)
and
U^{-}_{\hat{\sigma}}
defined by
(
3.76
)
on the right-hand side of (
3.79
) to update
\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
.
This defines the iteration map
\mathcal{Q}
from
V
to
\tilde{V}
, except that we discuss below
how the boundary value problem for (
3.78
)
with the boundary conditions (
3.84
) and (
3.88
) is solved
in
\mathbb{D}
.

As we discussed above, we perform the iteration in the spaces from
(
3.68
) for Problem
3.2
(ST)
and similar norms with appropriate growths for Problem
3.2
(WT),
expressed in the
\mathbf{z}
–coordinates
(
3.75
).
We focus below on the case of Problem
3.2
(WT), since the other case is similar.

For
\tau>0
, define

(3.89)

\begin{split}\Sigma^{\tau}_{1}&=\{v\,:\;\|v\|^{(-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(0,1+\beta);\mathbb{D}}+\|v_{z_{1}}\|^{(1-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,1);\mathbb{D}}\leq\tau\},\\[2.84526pt]
\Sigma^{\tau}_{2}&=\{v\,:\;\|v\|^{(-\alpha;O)(-1-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\mathbb{D}}\leq\tau\},\qquad\Sigma^{\tau}_{3}=\{v\,:\;\|v\|^{(-\alpha;0)}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta);\mathbb{R}^{+}}\leq\tau\},\\[2.84526pt]
\Sigma^{\tau}&=\{(\delta U_{1},\delta U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\,:\;\delta U_{1}\in\Sigma^{\tau}_{1}\times\Sigma^{\tau}_{1},\;\;\delta U_{2}\in\Sigma^{\tau}_{2}\times\Sigma^{\tau}_{2},\;\;\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\tau}_{3}\}.\end{split}

The condition on
v_{z_{1}}
in
\Sigma^{\tau}_{1}
is added for technical reasons.

It remains to discuss how we find

(\delta\tilde{w},\delta\tilde{p})\in\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}_{2}\times\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}_{2}

that solves (
3.78
) in
\mathbb{D}

with the boundary conditions (
3.84
) and (
3.88
). From system (
3.78
), we obtain

(3.90)

\displaystyle(\delta\tilde{p})_{z_{1}}=\frac{\lambda_{R}-\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}}{h\lambda_{I}}(\delta\tilde{w})_{z_{1}}+\frac{(\lambda_{R}-\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})^{2}+\lambda_{I}^{2}}{h\lambda_{I}}(\delta\tilde{w})_{z_{2}},

(3.91)

\displaystyle(\delta\tilde{p})_{z_{2}}=-\frac{1}{h\lambda_{I}}(\delta\tilde{w})_{z_{1}}-\frac{\lambda_{R}-\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}}{h\lambda_{I}}(\delta\tilde{w})_{z_{2}}.

Now, differentiating and subtracting the equations, we eliminate
\delta\tilde{p}

to obtain a second-order PDE for
\delta\tilde{w}
of the form:

(3.92)

\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}(a_{ij}(\delta\tilde{w})_{z_{j}})_{z_{i}}=0,

where the coefficients are computed explicitly from (
3.90
)–(
3.91
).
Note that, at the subsonic background solution (
3.59
), we have

\lambda_{R0}=0,\quad\lambda_{I0}>0,\quad h_{0}>0,

where the left-hand sides are constants and
\delta\hat{\sigma}_{0}=0
.
Then, computing the coefficients at the
background solution, equation (
3.92
) becomes

\frac{1}{\lambda_{I0}}(\delta\tilde{w})_{z_{1}z_{1}}+\lambda_{I0}(\delta\tilde{w})_{z_{2}z_{2}}=0,

that is, the equation is uniformly elliptic.
Then, for the coefficients computed
at
(U^{+}_{10}+\delta U_{1},U^{+}_{20}+\delta U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})
for

(\delta U_{1},\delta U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}
,
equation (
3.92
) is uniformly elliptic if
\varepsilon
is small. This
allows us to obtain the unique solution
\delta\tilde{w}\in\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}_{2}
of
(
3.92
) in
\mathbb{D}
with the boundary conditions (
3.84
) and (
3.88
).
Note that the inclusion

\delta\tilde{w}\in\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}_{2}
involves the asymptotic condition at infinity,
which makes the boundary value problem well-defined and allows us to prove the uniqueness.
After
\delta\tilde{w}
is determined,
we determine
\delta\tilde{p}
by the
z_{2}
–integration from (
3.91
) with the initial
condition (
3.84
), where it can be shown that
b_{1}\neq 0
. Then we show
that
\delta p\in\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}_{2}
. This completes the definition of the iteration map.

The iteration set for Problem
3.2
(WT) is
\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}
.
We first show that

\mathcal{Q}(\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon})\subset\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}
when
\varepsilon
is small,
and then obtain a fixed point by the Schauder fixed point theorem,
via showing that
\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}
is a compact subset in the Banach space
defined by replacing
\alpha
via
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha)
in the norms used
in the definition of
\Sigma^{\tau}

and showing that map
\mathcal{Q}
is continuous
in this norm.

3.
Asymptotic limit of the fixed point in the
\mathbf{y}
–coordinates
.
Let
(\delta U_{1},\delta U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\in\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}

be a fixed point of the iteration map, and let

(U_{1},U_{2},\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})=(U^{+}_{10}+\delta U_{1},U^{+}_{20}+\delta U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})
.

We change from the
\mathbf{z}
– to
\mathbf{y}
–coordinates by inverting (
3.75
):

\mathbf{z}:=(z_{1},z_{2})\,\to\,\mathbf{y}:=(y_{1},y_{2})=(z_{1},z_{2}+\delta\hat{\sigma}(z_{1})).

Since
\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{C_{0}\varepsilon}_{3}
, then both (
3.75
) and its inverse are close
to the identity map in
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}};\mathbb{R}^{2})

and
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathbb{D};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, respectively.
Then it follows that, in the
\mathbf{y}
–coordinates,

(\delta U_{1},\delta U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\in\tilde{\Sigma}^{2C_{0}\varepsilon}
if

\varepsilon
is small, where

(3.93)

\begin{split}\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{1}&=\{v\,:\;\|v\|^{(-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(0,1+\beta);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}+\|v_{z_{1}}\|^{(1-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,1);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}\leq\tau\},\quad\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{2}=\{v\,:\;\|v\|^{(-\alpha;O)(-1-\alpha;\mathcal{L}_{1})}_{2,\alpha;(1+\beta,0);\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}}\leq\tau\},\\[2.84526pt]
\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}&=\{(\delta U_{1},\delta U_{2},\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime})\,:\;\delta U_{1}\in\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{1}\times\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{1},\;\;\delta U_{2}\in\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{2}\times\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{2},\;\;\delta\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\tau}_{3}\}.\end{split}

In particular, this leads to the estimates of the second and third terms on the left-hand side of
(
3.67
).

Note that, if
v\in\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{2}
, then
v\to 0
as
|\mathbf{y}|\to\infty
in
\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}
,
with decay rate
|\mathbf{y}|^{-(\beta+1)}
.
However, for
v\in\tilde{\Sigma}^{\tau}_{1}
,
no asymptotic limit as
|\mathbf{y}|\to\infty
in
\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}
is defined.

Then, from (
3.59
)–(
3.60
),
it follows that
U_{2}=(w,p)\to(0,p_{0}^{+})
as
|\mathbf{y}|\to\infty
in
\mathbb{D}
;
however, for
U_{1}=(u_{1},\rho)
, the limit is not determined by space
\Sigma^{\tau}_{1}
,
and
(u_{1},\rho)
does not converge to
(u_{10}^{+},\rho_{0}^{+})
in general,
as we see below. Thus, we have to determine the limiting profiles

(u_{1}^{\infty}(y_{2}),\rho^{\infty}(y_{2}))
.

To determine
\rho^{\infty}(y_{2})
, we first obtain (
3.36
) from (
3.7
)–(
3.10
),
which implies (
3.37
).
Since
\hat{\sigma}(y_{1})
is determined,
then
A(y_{2})
in (
3.37
) is determined by the upstream state

U^{-}(\mathbf{y})
from the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions (
3.27
)–(
3.30
).
Then,
noting that
p\to p_{0}^{\infty}
, we obtain formally

\rho\to\rho^{\infty}(y_{2})=\Big(\frac{p_{0}^{+}}{A(y_{2})}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\qquad\mbox{as $|\mathbf{y}|\to\infty$ in $\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}$}.

Similarly, we use (
3.70
) to obtain

u_{1}\to u_{1}^{\infty}(y_{2})=\sqrt{2\Big(B(y_{2})-\frac{\gamma p_{0}^{+}}{(\gamma-1)\rho^{\infty}(y_{2})}\Big)}\qquad\mbox{as $|\mathbf{y}|\to\infty$ in $\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}$}.

Defining
\mathcal{U}^{\infty}(y_{2})=(u_{1}^{\infty}(y_{2}),0,p_{0}^{+},\rho^{\infty}(y_{2}))
, we can show that
the estimates of the first and the last terms on the left-hand side of (
3.67
)
hold. This completes the argument for case (i) of Theorem
3.3
.

Case (ii) is handled similarly. Note that the slower decay at infinity for case (ii),
i.e.
,

|\mathbf{y}|^{-\beta}
, is from the elliptic estimates, even if the faster decay at infinity in
(
3.25
) is required.
The reason for the difference in the rates for cases (i) and (ii) is
(
3.86
).

4.
Return to the
\mathbf{x}
-coordinates
.
We obtain Theorem
3.2
directly from Theorem
3.3
by changing the coordinates.
Recall that, when the Lagrangian coordinates are defined for Theorem
3.3
,
we have used the rotated coordinates
\mathbf{x}
in (
3.6
);
see the discussion in the paragraph before Theorem
3.3
.

From the estimates in Theorem
3.3
, it follows that, in the Lagrangian coordinates,

|U-U_{0}^{+}|\leq C\varepsilon
in
\mathbb{D}_{\hat{\sigma}}
, where
C
depends only on
(U_{0}^{-},U_{0}^{+})
.
Thus, the same is true in the
\mathbf{x}
–coordinates in
\Omega
.
Then it follows from (
3.5
)–(
3.6
) and (
3.59
) for positive
u_{10}^{+}
and
\rho_{0}^{+}

that the change of coordinates
\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{y}
given by (
3.6
) is bi-Lipschitz.
Then (
3.66
) follows from (
3.68
) directly.

Similarly, the estimates of the second and third terms on the left-hand side of
(
3.64
) follow from (
3.67
) directly.
In order to obtain the estimates of the remaining terms on the left-hand side of
(
3.64
), we need to identify
U^{\infty}(x_{2})
.

Note that, on shock
\mathcal{S}
, using (
3.6
) and the estimate of the third term
on the left-hand side of
(
3.64
), we see that, for small
\varepsilon
,

\partial_{{\bm{\tau}}_{\mathcal{S}}}\psi=\rho\mathbf{u}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathcal{S}}\geq\rho\mathbf{u}_{0}^{+}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}-C\varepsilon\geq\frac{1}{2}\rho\mathbf{u}_{0}^{+}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}>0.

Recall also that
\psi({\bf 0})=0
by (
3.5
).
Then, for each
y_{2}>0
, there exists a unique
\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}(y_{2})=(x_{1}^{\rm in}(y_{2}),x_{2}^{\rm in}(y_{2}))\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\psi(\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}(y_{2}))=y_{2}
and

\|\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}\|_{C^{2,\alpha}([0,\infty))}\leq C,\qquad\,\,(\mathbf{x}^{\rm in})^{\prime}\geq C^{-1}>0\;\;\mbox{ on $[0,\infty)$}.

From this and (
3.5
), it follows that, for each
y_{2}>0
,

\Omega\cap\{\mathbf{x}\,:\,\psi(\mathbf{x})=y_{2}\}=\{(x_{1},x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};y_{2}))\;:\;x_{1}>x_{1}^{\rm in}(y_{2})\},

where
x_{2}^{*}(\cdot;y_{2})
is the solution of the initial value problem for the differential equation:

(3.94)

\begin{cases}\partial_{x_{1}}x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};y_{2})=w(x_{1},x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};y_{2})),\\[2.84526pt]
x_{2}^{*}(x_{1}^{\rm in}(y_{2});y_{2})=x_{2}^{\rm in}(y_{2}),\end{cases}

where
w=\frac{u_{2}}{u_{1}}
(
cf
. (
3.60
)).
Since we have obtained the estimate
of the second term on the left-hand side of
(
3.24
), using (
3.59
), we have

(3.95)

|D^{k}w(\mathbf{x})|\leq C_{0}\varepsilon(1+|\mathbf{x}|)^{-1-\beta}\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega,\;\;$ for $k=0,1,2$}.

In particular, for each
y_{2}\geq 0
and
k=0,1,2
,

(3.96)

\int_{x_{1}^{\rm in}(y_{2})}^{\infty}|D^{k}w(x_{1},x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};y_{2}))|\,{\rm d}x_{1}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}(1+x_{1})^{-1-\beta}\,{\rm d}x_{1}\leq C\varepsilon.

Applying this with
k=1
, we conclude that
\lim_{x_{1}\to\infty}x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};y_{2})
exists for each
y_{2}\geq 0
,
which is denoted as
x_{2}^{\infty}(y_{2})
.

Recall the structure of
\Omega
in (
3.62
),
where
b_{\rm rot}(x_{1})\to 0
and
\tilde{\sigma}(x_{1})\to\infty
as
x_{1}\to\infty

by (
3.63
) and the estimate of the third term in (
3.64
).
Differentiating (
3.94
) twice with respect to
y_{2}
and using
the
C^{2}
–estimate of
\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}
and (
3.96
),
we obtain that

\|x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};\cdot)\|_{C^{2}([b_{\rm rot}(x_{1}),\tilde{\sigma}(x_{1})])}\leq C
for each
x_{1}>0
.
From this, we have

(3.97)

\mbox{$x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};\cdot)\to x_{2}^{\infty}(\cdot)$ in $C^{1}$ on compact subsets on $[0,\infty)$
as $x_{1}\to\infty$},

with
\|x_{2}^{\infty}\|_{C^{2}([0,\infty))}\leq C
. Also, by a similar argument, using the
C^{2,\alpha}
–regularity of
\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}

and the estimate of
w
in the second term in (
3.64
), we obtain
that
x_{2}^{\infty}\in C^{2,\alpha}([0,\infty))
.

Furthermore, for the background solution, the potential functions
\psi_{0}^{-}
of
U_{0}^{-}
,

\psi_{0}^{+}
of
U_{0}^{+}
, and
\psi_{0}
of the transonic shock solution
(U_{0}^{-},U_{0}^{+})
in
\{x_{1}>0,x_{2}>0\}
are:

\psi_{0}^{-}(\mathbf{x})=\rho^{-}_{0}u^{-}_{10}(x_{1}-x_{2}\tan\theta_{\rm w}),\quad\psi_{0}^{+}(\mathbf{x})=\rho^{+}_{0}u^{+}_{10}x_{1},\quad\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{cases}\psi_{0}^{-}(\mathbf{x})\;\;\;\mbox{ if }x_{2}<\tilde{s}x_{1},\\[1.42262pt]
\psi_{0}^{+}(\mathbf{x})\;\;\;\mbox{ if }x_{2}>\tilde{s}x_{1},\end{cases}

by using (
3.59
), where
\psi_{0}
is Lipschitz. Then, estimating
\psi-\psi_{0}^{-}
in
\Omega^{-}
via
(
3.63
) (where the polynomial decay is of degree
-(1+\beta)
so
that the calculations similar to (
3.96
) can be used)
and using the Rankine-Hugoniot
conditions on
\mathcal{S}
, we obtain

|(\mathbf{x}^{\rm in})^{\prime}-(\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}_{0})^{\prime}|\leq C\varepsilon\qquad\mbox{ on $[0,\infty)$},

where
\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}_{0}(y_{2})=\frac{y_{2}}{\rho^{+}_{0}u^{+}_{10}}(1,\tilde{s}_{0})

that is the corresponding function
\mathbf{x}^{\rm in}
of the background solution.

Denote by
x^{*}_{20}(x_{1};y_{2})
the corresponding function
x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};y_{2})
of the background solution.
Then

x^{*}_{20}(x_{1};y_{2})=\frac{y_{2}}{\rho^{+}_{0}u^{+}_{10}}\qquad\,\mbox{on $x_{1}>\frac{y_{2}}{\rho^{+}_{0}u^{+}_{10}\tilde{s}_{0}}$ for each $y_{2}\geq 0$}.

Thus,
x^{*}_{20}(x_{1};y_{2})
is independent of
x_{1}
, so that
x^{*}_{20}(x_{1};y_{2})=x^{*}_{20}(y_{2})
.
Then, denoting

g(x_{1};y_{2})=x^{*}_{2}(x_{1};y_{2})-x^{*}_{20}(y_{2}),

we see that
g
satisfies

(3.98)

\begin{cases}\partial_{x_{1}}g(x_{1};y_{2})=w(x_{1},x_{2}^{*}(x_{1};y_{2})),\\[2.84526pt]
|g(x_{1}^{\rm in}(y_{2});y_{2})|\leq C\varepsilon.\end{cases}

From this and (
3.96
)–(
3.97
),
we obtain that
|(x_{2}^{\infty})^{\prime}-(x_{20}^{\infty})^{\prime}|\leq C\varepsilon
,
where
(x_{20}^{\infty})^{\prime}(y_{2})=(x^{*}_{20})^{\prime}(y_{2})=\frac{1}{\rho^{+}_{0}u^{+}_{10}}
.
Therefore, we have

(x_{2}^{\infty})^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{2\rho^{+}_{0}u^{+}_{10}}\quad\mbox{ on $[0,\infty)$},

if
\varepsilon
is small. In particular, noting that
x_{2}^{\infty}(0)=0
since
\partial W
is a
streamline corresponding to
\psi=0
and
\lim_{x_{1}\to\infty}b_{\rm rot}(x_{1})=0
by (
3.63
), we obtain

x_{2}^{\infty}([0,\infty))=[0,\infty)
.
Then there exists the inverse function
y_{2}^{*}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
to
x_{2}^{\infty}(\cdot)
such that

y_{2}^{*}\in C^{2,\alpha}([0,\infty))

with
y_{2}^{*}(0)=0
and

(y_{2}^{*})^{\prime}\geq\frac{1}{C}>0
.

Finally, defining
U^{\infty}(x_{2})=\mathcal{U}^{\infty}(y_{2}^{*}(x_{2}))
, we obtain
(
3.64
) from (
3.67
).

For more details, see Chen-Chen-Feldman
[
25
]
.

Remark 3.1
.

For the global stability of weak transonic shocks for the
3
-D wedge problem,
see
[
28
,
26
]
; also see the instability phenomenon for
strong transonic shocks for the
3
-D wedge problem in
[
77
]
.
For the global stability of conical shocks for the M-D conic problem,
see
[
27
]
for the transonic shock case
and
[
38
,
49
,
82
]
for the supersonic shock case.

4.
Two-Dimensional Transonic Shocks and Free Boundary Problems
for the Self-Similar Euler Equations for Potential Flow

In §2–§3, we have discussed the free boundary problems for steady transonic shock solutions of the compressible Euler equations.
Now we discuss free boundary problems for time-dependent solutions.

Time-dependent solutions with shocks of the Cauchy problem for the compressible Euler system
may exhibit non-uniqueness in general; see
[
50
,
71
]
and the references cited therein.
On the other hand, many fundamental physical phenomena, including shock reflection/diffraction,
are determined by the time-dependent solutions of self-similar structure;
moreover, the uniqueness can be established in a carefully chosen class
of self-similar solutions with shocks.
In this section, we focus on this case; more precisely, we describe transonic shocks and free boundary problems
for self-similar shock reflection/diffraction for the Euler equations for potential flow.

The two-dimensional compressible potential flow is governed by the conservation law of mass and the Bernoulli law
for the density function
\rho
and the velocity potential
\Phi
(
i.e.
,
\mathbf{u}=\nabla\Phi
):

(4.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+\nabla_{\bf x}\cdot(\rho\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi)=0,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi|^{2}+h(\rho)=B

for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}:=(0,\infty)
and
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
where
B
is the Bernoulli constant,
and
h(\rho)
is given by

(4.3)

h(\rho)=\frac{\rho^{\gamma-1}-1}{\gamma-1}\qquad\,\,\mbox{for the adiabatic exponent $\gamma>1$.}

By (
4.2
)–(
4.3
),

\rho
can be expressed as

(4.4)

\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi)=h^{-1}(B-\partial_{t}\Phi-\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi|^{2}).

Then system (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) can be rewritten as the following second-order nonlinear wave equation:

(4.5)

\partial_{t}\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi)+\nabla_{\bf x}\cdot\big(\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi)\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi\big)=0

with
\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,\nabla_{\bf x}\Phi)
determined by (
4.4
).

Note that equation (
4.4
) is invariant under the self-similar scaling:

(4.6)

(t,{\bf x})\rightarrow(\alpha t,\alpha{\bf x}),\quad\Phi\rightarrow\frac{\Phi}{\alpha}\qquad\quad\text{for}\;\;\alpha\neq 0,

and thus it admits self-similar solutions in the form of

(4.7)

\Phi(t,{\bf x})=t\phi({\bm{\xi}})\qquad\quad\text{for}\;\;{\bm{\xi}}=\frac{{\bf x}}{t}.

Then the pseudo-potential function

\varphi({\bm{\xi}})=\phi({\bm{\xi}})-\frac{1}{2}|{\bm{\xi}}|^{2}

satisfies
the following equation:

(4.8)

{\rm div}(\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)D\varphi)+2\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)=0

for

(4.9)

\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)=\bigl(B_{0}-(\gamma-1)(\frac{1}{2}|D\varphi|^{2}+\varphi)\bigr)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}},

where
B_{0}=(\gamma-1)B+1
,
and the divergence
{\rm div}
and gradient
D
are with respect to
{\bm{\xi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Equation (
4.8
)
written in the non-divergence form is

(4.10)

(c^{2}-\varphi_{{\xi_{1}}}^{2})\varphi_{{\xi_{1}}{\xi_{1}}}-2\varphi_{\xi_{1}}\varphi_{\xi_{2}}\varphi_{{\xi_{1}}{\xi_{2}}}+(c^{2}-\varphi_{{\xi_{2}}}^{2})\varphi_{{\xi_{2}}{\xi_{2}}}+2c^{2}-|D\varphi|^{2}=0,

where the sonic speed
c=c(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)
is determined by

(4.11)

c^{2}(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)=\rho^{\gamma-1}(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)=B_{0}-(\gamma-1)\big(\frac{1}{2}|D\varphi|^{2}+\varphi\big).

Another form of (
4.10
), which uses both the potential
\phi
and
the pseudo-potential
\varphi
, is

(4.12)

(c^{2}-\varphi_{\xi_{1}}^{2})\phi_{\xi_{1}\xi_{1}}-2\varphi_{\xi_{1}}\varphi_{\xi_{2}}\phi_{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}+(c^{2}-\varphi_{\xi_{2}}^{2})\phi_{\xi_{2}\xi_{2}}=0.

Equation (
4.8
) is a nonlinear PDE of mixed elliptic-hyperbolic type. It is elliptic
at
{\bm{\xi}}
if and only if

(4.13)

|D\varphi|<c(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)\qquad\,\,\mbox{at ${\bm{\xi}}$},

and is hyperbolic if the opposite inequality holds.
This can be seen more clearly from the rotational invariance of (
4.10
),
by fixing
{\bm{\xi}}
and choosing coordinates
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
so that
\xi_{1}
is along the direction of
D\varphi({\bm{\xi}})
.

Moreover, from (
4.10
)–(
4.11
),
equation (
4.8
) satisfies the Galilean invariance property:
If
\varphi({\bm{\xi}})
is a solution, then its shift
\varphi({\bm{\xi}}-{\bm{\xi}}_{0})
for any constant vector
{\bm{\xi}}_{0}
is also a solution.
Furthermore,
\varphi({\bm{\xi}})+{\it const.}
is a solution of (
4.8
)
with adjusted constant
B
correspondingly in (
4.9
) and (
4.11
).

One class of solutions of (
4.8
)
is that of
constant states
that are the solutions
with constant velocity
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
This implies that the pseudo-potential of a constant state satisfies

D\varphi=\mathbf{v}-{\bm{\xi}}
so that

(4.14)

\varphi({\bm{\xi}})=-\frac{1}{2}|{\bm{\xi}}|^{2}+\mathbf{v}\cdot{\bm{\xi}}+C,

where
C
is a constant. For such
\varphi
, the expressions
in (
4.9
) and (
4.11
)
imply that the density and sonic
speed are positive constants
\rho
and
c
,
i.e.
,
independent of
{\bm{\xi}}
.
Then, from (
2.4
)
and (
4.14
),
the ellipticity condition for the constant state is

|{\bm{\xi}}-\mathbf{v}|<c.

Thus, for a constant state
\mathbf{v}
,
equation (
4.8
)
is elliptic inside the
sonic circle
,
with center
\mathbf{v}
and radius
c
, and hyperbolic outside this circle.

We also note that,
if density
\rho
is a constant, then the solution is a constant state;
that is, the corresponding
pseudo-potential
\varphi
is of form (
4.14
).

Since the problem involves transonic shocks, we have to consider
weak solutions of equation (
4.8
),
which admit shocks.
As in
[
33
]
,
the weak solutions are defined in the distributional sense
in a domain
\Lambda
in the
{\bm{\xi}}
–coordinates.

Definition 4.1
.

A function
\varphi\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}(\Lambda)
is called a weak solution of
(
4.8
) if

(i)

B_{0}-(\gamma-1)(\frac{1}{2}|D\varphi|^{2}+\varphi)\geq 0\quad\text{a.e. in }\Lambda
,

(ii)

(\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi),\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)|D\varphi|)\in(L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Lambda))^{2}
,

(iii)

For every
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\Lambda)
,

(4.15)

\int_{\Lambda}\big(\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)D\varphi\cdot D\zeta-2\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)\zeta\big)\mathrm{d}{\bm{\xi}}=0.

A shock is a curve across which
D\varphi
is discontinuous. If
\Lambda^{+}
and
\Lambda^{-}(:=\Lambda\setminus\overline{\Lambda^{+}})

are two nonempty open subsets of a domain
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
\mathcal{S}:=\partial\Lambda^{+}\cap\Lambda
is a
C^{1}
-curve
where
D\varphi
has a jump, then
\varphi\in C^{1}(\Lambda^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{S})\cap C^{2}(\Lambda^{\pm})

is a global weak solution of (
4.8
) in
\Lambda
if and only if
\varphi
is in
W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\Lambda)

and satisfies equation (
4.8
) and the Rankine-Hugoniot condition on
\mathcal{S}
:

(4.16)

\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)D\varphi\cdot{\bm{\nu}}|_{\Lambda^{+}\cap\mathcal{S}}=\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)D\varphi\cdot{\bm{\nu}}|_{\Lambda^{-}\cap\mathcal{S}}.

Note that the condition
\varphi\in W^{1,\infty}_{\rm loc}(\Lambda)
requires that

(4.17)

\varphi_{\Lambda^{+}\cap\mathcal{S}}=\varphi_{\Lambda^{-}\cap\mathcal{S}},

which is consistent with
{\rm curl}(D\varphi)=0
in the distributional sense.

A piecewise smooth solution with the discontinuities is called an
entropy solution

of (
4.8
) if it satisfies the entropy condition: density
\rho
increases in the pseudo-flow direction of

D\varphi_{\Lambda^{+}\cap\mathcal{S}}
across any discontinuity. Then such a discontinuity is called a shock.

4.1.
The von Neumann Problem for Shock Reflection-Diffraction

We now describe the von Neumann problem for shock reflection-diffraction, proposed for mathematical analysis first
in
[
105
,
106
,
107
]
.
When a vertical planar shock perpendicular to the flow
direction
x_{1}
and separating two uniform states (0) and (1),
with constant velocities

\mathbf{u}_{0}=(0,0)
and
\mathbf{u}_{1}=(u_{1},0)

and constant densities
\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}

(state (0) is ahead or to the right of the shock, and state
(1) is behind the shock), hits a symmetric wedge:

W:=\big\{(x_{1},x_{2})\,:\,|x_{2}|<x_{1}\tan\theta_{\rm w},x_{1}>0\big\}

head-on at time
t=0
,
a reflection-diffraction process takes place when
t>0
.
Then a fundamental question is what types of wave patterns of
reflection-diffraction configurations may be formed around the wedge.
The complexity of reflection-diffraction configurations was first reported
by Ernst Mach
[
89
]
in 1878, who first observed two patterns of
reflection-diffraction configurations:
Regular reflection (two-shock configuration; see
e.g.

Figs.
4.2
–
4.2
)
and Mach reflection (three-shock/one-vortex-sheet configuration);
also see
[
8
,
35
,
52
,
103
]
.
The issues remained dormant until the 1940s when John von Neumann
[
105
,
106
,
107
]
,
as well as other mathematical/experimental
scientists (
cf.

[
8
,
35
,
52
,
63
,
103
]

and the references cited therein),
began extensive research into all aspects of shock reflection-diffraction phenomena,
due to its importance in applications.
It has been found that the situations are much more complicated than
what Mach originally observed: The Mach reflection can be further
divided into more specific sub-patterns, and various other patterns of
shock reflection-diffraction configurations may occur such as the double Mach reflection,
the von Neumann reflection, and the Guderley reflection;
see
[
8
,
35
,
52
,
63
,
65
,
103
]

and the references cited therein.
Then the fundamental scientific issues include:

(i)

Structures of the shock reflection-diffraction configurations;

(ii)

Transition criteria among the different patterns of shock
reflection-diffraction configurations;

(iii)

Dependence of the patterns upon the physical parameters such as the
wedge angle
\theta_{\rm w}
, the incident-shock-wave Mach number, and the
adiabatic exponent
\gamma>1
.

In particular, several transition criteria among the different
patterns of shock reflection-diffraction configurations have been proposed,
including the sonic conjecture and the detachment conjecture by von Neumann

[
105
,
106
,
107
]
.

A careful asymptotic analysis has been made for various reflection-diffraction
configurations in Lighthill
[
83
,
84
]
,
Keller-Blank
[
69
]
, Hunter-Keller
[
68
]
, Harabetian
[
67
]
,
Morawetz
[
93
]
, and the references
cited therein; also see Glimm-Majda
[
63
]
.
Large or small scale numerical simulations have been also performed;

cf.

[
8
,
63
,
110
]
and the references cited therein.
However, most of the fundamental issues for shock reflection-diffraction
phenomena have not been understood, especially the global structure and
transition between the different patterns of shock reflection-diffraction configurations.
This is partially because physical and numerical experiments are
hampered by many difficulties and have not yielded clear transition criteria
between the different patterns. In particular, numerical dissipation or physical
viscosity smear the shocks and cause boundary layers that interact with
the reflection-diffraction patterns and can cause spurious Mach steams;
cf.

[
110
]
. Furthermore, some different patterns occur when
the wedge angles are only fractions of a degree apart,
a resolution as yet unreachable even by
sophisticated
experiments
(
cf.

[
8
,
LD
]
).
For this reason, it is impossible to
distinguish experimentally between the sonic and detachment criteria clearly,
as pointed out in
[
8
]
.
In this regard, the necessary approach to understand
fully the shock reflection-diffraction phenomena, especially the transition criteria,
is via rigorous mathematical analysis.
To achieve this, it is essential to formulate the shock reflection-diffraction problem
as a free boundary problem and establish the global existence, regularity,
and structural stability of its solution.

Mathematically, the shock reflection-diffraction problem
is a two-dimensional lateral Riemann problem in domain

\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\overline{W}
.

Problem 4.1

(Two-Dimensional Lateral Riemann Problem)
.

Piecewise constant initial data, consisting of state
(0)
with velocity
\mathbf{u}_{0}=(0,0)

on
\{x_{1}>0\}\setminus\overline{W}

and state
(1)
with velocity
\mathbf{u}_{1}=(u_{1},0)

on
\{x_{1}<0\}
connected by a shock at
x_{1}=0
,
are prescribed at
t=0
.
Seek a solution of the Euler system (
4.1
)–(
4.2
) for
t\geq 0
subject to these initial
data and the boundary condition
\nabla\Phi\cdot{\bm{\nu}}=0
on
\partial W
.

In order to define the notion of weak solutions of Problem
4.1
, it is noted that
the boundary condition can be written as
\rho\nabla\Phi\cdot{\bm{\nu}}=0
on
\partial W
,
which is spatial conormal to equation (
4.5
).
Then we have

Definition 4.2

(Weak Solutions of Problem
4.1
)
.

A function

\Phi\in W^{1,1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus W))

is called a weak
solution of
Problem
4.1

if
\Phi
satisfies the following properties
:

(i)

B-\left(\partial_{t}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2}\right)\geq h(0+)
a.e. in

\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus W)
.

(ii)

For
\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi)
determined by (
4.4
),

(\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2}),\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2})|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|)\in(L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus W}))^{2}.

(iii)

For every
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\overline{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus W}\Big(\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2})\partial_{t}\zeta+\rho(\partial_{t}\Phi,|\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\Phi|^{2})\nabla\Phi\cdot\nabla\zeta\Big)\,{\rm d}\mathbf{x}{\rm d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus W}\rho(0,\mathbf{x})\zeta(0,\mathbf{x}){\rm d}\mathbf{x}=0,

where

\rho|_{t=0}=\begin{cases}\rho_{0}\qquad\,\,&\mbox{for $|x_{2}|>x_{1}\tan\theta_{\rm w}$ and $x_{1}>0$},\\
\rho_{1}\qquad\,\,&\mbox{for}\,\,x_{1}<0.\end{cases}

Remark 4.3
.

Since
\zeta
does not need to be zero on
\partial\Lambda
,
the integral identity in Definition
4.2

is a weak form of equation
(
4.5
)

and the boundary condition
\rho\nabla\Phi\cdot{\bm{\nu}}=0
on
\partial W
.

Remark 4.4
.

A weak solution is called an entropy solution if it satisfies the entropy condition
that is consistent with the second law of thermodynamics

(
cf.
[
35
,
52
,
53
,
73
]
)
. In particular, a piecewise smooth solution
is an entropy solution if the discontinuities are all shocks.

Notice that Problem
4.1

is invariant under scaling (
4.6
), so it admits self-similar solutions determined by
equation (
4.8
) with (
4.9
), along with the appropriate boundary conditions,
through (
4.7
).
We now show how such solutions
in self-similar coordinates
{\bm{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\frac{{\bf x}}{t}

can be constructed.

First, by the symmetry of the problem with respect to the
\xi_{1}
–axis,
we consider only the upper half-plane

\{\xi_{2}>0\}
and prescribe the boundary condition:
\varphi_{{\bm{\nu}}}=0

on the symmetry line
\{\xi_{2}=0\}
.
Note that state (1) satisfies this condition.
Then Problem
4.1
is reformulated as a boundary value problem
in the unbounded domain:

\Lambda:=\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus\{{\bm{\xi}}\,:\,|\xi_{2}|\leq\xi_{1}\tan\theta_{\rm w},\xi_{1}>0\}

in the self-similar coordinates
{\bm{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, where
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:=\mathbb{R}^{2}\cap\{\xi_{2}>0\}
.
The incident shock in
the self-similar coordinates is the half-line
\mathcal{S}_{0}=\{\xi_{1}=\xi_{1}^{0}\}\cap\Lambda
, where

(4.18)

\xi_{1}^{0}=\rho_{1}\sqrt{\frac{2(c_{1}^{2}-c_{0}^{2})}{(\gamma-1)(\rho_{1}^{2}-\rho_{0}^{2})}}=\frac{\rho_{1}u_{1}}{\rho_{1}-\rho_{0}},

which is determined by the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions between states (0) and (1) on
\mathcal{S}_{0}
.
Then Problem
4.1
for self-similar solutions becomes the following problem:

Problem 4.2

(Boundary Value Problem)
.

Seek a
solution
\varphi
of equation (
4.8
)–(
4.9
)
in the self-similar
domain
\Lambda
with the slip boundary condition

D\varphi\cdot{\bm{\nu}}|_{\partial\Lambda}=0

and the asymptotic boundary condition at infinity
:

\varphi\to\bar{\varphi}=\begin{cases}\varphi_{0}\qquad\mbox{for}\,\,\,\xi_{1}>\xi_{1}^{0},\xi_{2}>\xi_{1}\tan\theta_{\rm w},\\
\varphi_{1}\qquad\mbox{for}\,\,\,\xi_{1}<\xi_{1}^{0},\;\xi_{2}>0,\end{cases}\qquad\mbox{when $|{\bm{\xi}}|\to\infty$,}

where
\varphi_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}|{\bm{\xi}}|^{2}
and
\varphi_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}|{\bm{\xi}}|^{2}+u_{1}(\xi_{1}-\xi^{0}_{1})
.

A weak solution of Problem
4.2
is obtained by the following modification
of Definition
4.1
:
(
4.15
) is now required to hold
for all
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
.
As discussed in Remark
4.3
,
with such a choice of function
\zeta
, the integral identity (
4.15
)
includes both equation (
4.8
) and the boundary condition of conormal form:

\rho D\varphi\cdot{\bm{\nu}}=0
on
\partial\Lambda
.
A weak solution is called entropy solution if it satisfies
the entropy condition:
density
\rho
increases
in the pseudo-flow direction of
D\varphi|_{\Lambda^{+}\cap\mathcal{S}}
across any discontinuity curve (
i.e.
, shock).

Figure 4.1.
Supersonic regular shock reflection-diffraction configuration

Figure 4.2.
Subsonic    regular shock reflection-diffraction configuration

Now we describe the more detailed structure of the regular reflection-diffraction configurations as shown
in Figs.
4.2
–
4.2
.
If a solution has one of the regular shock reflection-diffraction configurations,
and if its pseudo-potential

\varphi
is
C^{1}
in the subregion
\widehat{\Omega}

between the wedge and the reflected shock, then, at
P_{0}
,
it should satisfy both
the slip boundary condition on the wedge and the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions
with state
(1)
across the flat shock
\mathcal{S}_{1}=\{\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}\}
, which passes
through point
P_{0}
where the incident shock meets the wedge boundary.
Define the uniform state (2) with pseudo-potential
\varphi_{2}({\bm{\xi}})
such
that

\varphi_{2}(P_{0})=\varphi(P_{0}),\qquad D\varphi_{2}({P_{0}})=\lim_{P\to P_{0},\;P\in\widehat{\Omega}}D\varphi(P).

Then the constant density
\rho_{2}
of state (2) is equal to
\rho(|D\varphi|^{2},\varphi)({P_{0}})

defined by (
4.8
):

\rho_{2}=\rho(|D\varphi_{2}|^{2},\varphi_{2})({P_{0}}).

From the properties of
\varphi
discussed above, it follows that
D\varphi_{2}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}=0
on the wedge boundary and the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions
(
4.16
)–(
4.17
) hold on the flat shock
\mathcal{S}_{1}=\{\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}\}

between states (1) and (2), which passes through
{P_{0}}
.
In particular,
\varphi_{2}
satisfies the following three conditions at
P_{0}
:

(4.19)

D\varphi_{2}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}=0,\,\,\,\,\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{1},\,\,\,\,\rho(|D\varphi_{2}|^{2},\varphi_{2})D\varphi_{2}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}=\rho_{1}D\varphi_{1}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}\qquad\mbox{for ${\bm{\nu}}_{\mathcal{S}_{1}}=\frac{D(\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2})}{|D(\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2})|}$}.

where
{\bm{\nu}}_{\rm w}
is the outward normal to the wedge boundary.

The entropy solution
\varphi
, correspondingly state (2), can be either supersonic or subsonic at
{P_{0}}
.
This determines the supersonic or subsonic type of regular shock reflection-diffraction configurations.
The regular reflection solution in the supersonic region is expected to
consist of the constant states separated by straight shocks (
cf.

[
98
, Theorem 4.1]
).
Then, when state (2) is supersonic at
{P_{0}}
, it can be shown that the constant state (2), extended
up to arc
P_{1}P_{4}
of the sonic circle of state (2) between the wall and
the straight shock
P_{0}P_{1}\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}
separating it from state (1),
as shown in Fig.
4.2
,
satisfies equation (
4.8
) in the region,
the Rankine-Hugoniot condition (
4.16
)–(
4.17
